Berele row-insertion and quantum symmetric pairs
Hideya Watanabe

Abstract
. The Berele row-insertion is a symplectic analogue of the Schensted row-insertion. In the present paper, we provide it with a representation theoretical interpretation via the quantum symmetric pairs of type A ​ II A\mathrm{II} . As applications, we lift Berele’s Robinson–Schensted correspondence and Kobayashi–Matsumura’s Robinson–Schensted–Knuth (RSK for short) correspondence to isomorphisms of representations over a quantum symmetric pair coideal subalgebra, and establish the dual RSK correspondence of type A ​ II A\mathrm{II} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 17B37; Secondary 17B10, 05E10

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Row-insertion and the Berele row-insertion

It has been known for a long time that combinatorics of semistandard tableaux have intimate connections with representation theory of the general linear Lie algebras
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
over
\mathbb{C}
.
For example, the finite-dimensional irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
are parametrized by the partitions of length at most
n
, and the representation corresponding to a partition
\lambda
has a distinguished basis parametrized by the semistandard tableaux of shape
\lambda
with letters in
\{1,\dots,n\}
.

The row-insertion, discovered by Schensted, is an algorithm which takes a pair
(T,x)
consisting of a semistandard tableau
T
and a positive integer
x
as input, and outputs a new semistandard tableau
T\leftarrow x
.
From a representation theoretical point of view, this algorithm can be considered to describe the irreducible decompositions of the tensor products of a finite-dimensional irreducible representation and the vector representation of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
.
The row-insertion is a building block for other algorithms related to representation theory such as the Robinson–Schensted (RS for short) correspondence, Robinson–Schensted–Knuth (RSK for short) correspondence, and dual RSK correspondence (see e.g.,
[
Ful97
]
for details).

In representation theory of the symplectic Lie algebras
\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}
over
\mathbb{C}
, the symplectic tableaux (a.k.a. the King tableaux) parametrize bases of the finite-dimensional irreducible representations
[
Kin76
]
.

Berele
[
Ber86
]
discovered a symplectic analogue of the row-insertion.
This algorithm takes a pair
(T,x)
consisting of a symplectic tableau
T
and a positive integer
x
as input, and outputs a new symplectic tableau
T\xleftarrow{\mathrm{B}}x
.
As in the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
case, the Berele row-insertion describes the irreducible decompositions of the tensor products of a finite-dimensional irreducible representation and the vector representation of
\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}
.
In
[
Ber86
]
, Berele used this row-insertion to obtain an RS-type correspondence.
Recently, Kobayashi and Matsumura
[
KoMa25
]
established an RSK-type correspondence based on the Berele row-insertion.

1.2.
Quantum groups

Since the birth of the quantum groups (a.k.a. the quantized enveloping algebras) and crystals around 1990, various results in representation theory of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
have been quantized.
In particular, the algorithms mentioned above have turned out to be isomorphisms of crystals and have been lifted to isomorphisms of representations of the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
; see a survey paper
[
Kwo09
]
.

Based on crystal theory, Kashiwara and Nakashima
[
KaNa94
]
introduced new classes of tableaux for representation theory of
\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}
and the special orthogonal Lie algebras
\mathfrak{so}_{n}
.
In particular, the Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux of type
C
, i.e., for
\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}
, are different from the symplectic tableaux.
A row-insertion type algorithm for the Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux of type
C
was introduced and studied in
[
Lec02
]
.
Of course, it differs from the Berele row-insertion.

Thus, the roles of the symplectic tableaux and Berele row-insertion in the context of quantum groups have been missing.

1.3.
Quantum symmetric pairs

In a recent paper
[
Wat25
]
, it turned out that the symplectic tableaux naturally appear and work well in representation theory of the quantum symmetric pairs
(U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2n}),U^{\imath}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}))
of type
A\mathrm{II}
(see also
[
NSW25
]
).
Here,
U^{\imath}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})
is a quantization of
\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}
that is different from the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})
of type
C
.
Unlike
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})
, the algebra
U^{\imath}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})
is a subalgebra of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2n})
.
Therefore, each
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2n})
-module can be regarded as a
U^{\imath}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})
-module via the embedding.
By “representation theory of
(U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2n}),U^{\imath}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}))
”, we mean the study of such
U^{\imath}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})
-modules.
For a general theory of quantum symmetric pairs, we refer the reader to
[
Kol14
]
.

Let
\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
denote the set of partitions of length at most
n
.
The finite-dimensional irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}
are parametrized by
\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
.
Each such representation can be quantized to a finite-dimensional irreducible representation of
U^{\imath}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})

[
Mol06
]
,
[
Wat21
]
.
Let
V^{\imath}(\nu)
denote the corresponding representation.
It was proved in
[
Wat25
]
that
V^{\imath}(\nu)
has a distinguished basis of the form

\{b^{\imath}_{T}\mid T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)\},

where
Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)
denotes the set of symplectic tableaux of shape
\nu
.
This basis has several good properties in common with the canonical basis of a finite-dimensional irreducible representation
V(\lambda)
of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2n})
;
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
represents the highest weight of the representation.

In
[
Wat25
]
, an algorithm that transforms each semistandard tableau
T
into a symplectic tableau
P(T)
was introduced.
It describes the irreducible decompositions of the finite-dimensional irreducible representation of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2n})
as a
U^{\imath}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})
-module.
Hence, one can naturally define an analogue of the row-insertion by

T\xleftarrow{A\mathrm{II}}x:=P(T\leftarrow x).

We call it the row-insertion of type
A\mathrm{II}
.

1.4.
Results

The main result in the present paper is that the row-insertion of type
A\mathrm{II}
coincides with the Berele row-insertion (Theorem
5.4.3
):

T\xleftarrow{A\mathrm{II}}x=T\xleftarrow{\mathrm{B}}x.

This suggests that the symplectic tableaux and the Berele row-insertion should be thought of as a type
A\mathrm{II}
analogue of the semistandard tableaux and the Schensted row-insertion, but not type
C
.
We prove this result by rewriting the two algorithms in terms of the Schensted row-insertion and the Schützenberger sliding.

As applications, we lift the Berele RS correspondence and the Kobayashi–Matsumura RSK correspondence to
U^{\imath}(\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})
-isomorphisms (Theorems
6.1.3
and
6.2.5
).
Namely, there exist
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module isomorphisms

\mathrm{RS}^{A\mathrm{II}}:V^{\imath}(\nu)\otimes V(1)^{\otimes N}\to\bigoplus_{\xi\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}}(V^{\imath}(\xi)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(q)\mathrm{OT}_{n,N}(\nu,\xi))

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{RSK}^{A\mathrm{II}}:

\displaystyle V^{\imath}(\nu)\otimes V(l_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes V(l_{k})

\displaystyle\to\bigoplus_{\xi\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}}V^{\imath}(\xi)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(q)\{U\in\mathrm{CSOT}_{n,k}(\nu,\xi)\mid c(U)=(l_{1},\dots,l_{k})\}

which recovers the Berele RS correspondence and the Kobayashi–Matsumura RSK correspondence at
q=\infty
, respectively.
Here,
\mathrm{OT}_{n,k}(\nu,\xi)
denotes the set of
oscillating tableaux
(a.k.a.
up-down tableaux
) (Definition
6.1.2
) and
\mathrm{CSOT}_{n,k}(\nu,\xi)
the set of
column-strict oscillating tableaux
(Definition
6.2.2
).

Moreover, we establish the dual RSK correspondence of type
A\mathrm{II}
and its quantum lift (Theorem
6.3.5
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{dRSK}^{A\mathrm{II}}:

\displaystyle V^{\imath}(\nu)\otimes V(1^{k_{1}})\otimes\cdots\otimes V(1^{k_{l}})

\displaystyle\to\bigoplus_{\xi\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}}V^{\imath}(\xi)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(q)\{U\in\mathrm{RSOT}_{n,l}(\nu,\xi)\mid c(U)=(k_{1},\dots,k_{l})\}.

Here,
\mathrm{RSOT}_{n,k}(\nu,\xi)
denotes the set of
row-strict oscillating tableaux
(Definition
6.3.2
).

1.5.
Organization

This paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we prepare necessary notions regarding partitions such as horizontal strips, vertical strips, and punctured partitions.
Section
3
is devoted to reviewing the row-insertion and the sliding on semistandard tableaux.
In Section
4
, we study the Berele row-insertion, the central research object in the present paper.
We briefly review representation theory of the quantum symmetric pairs of type
A\mathrm{II}
in Section
5
.
We also prove the coincidence of the Berele row-insertion and the row-insertion of type
A\mathrm{II}
there.
In Section
6
, we use this result to deduce various combinatorial and representation theoretical results mentioned above.

1.6.
Acknowledgments

The author would like to thank the Research Institute for Mathematical Sciences, an International Joint Usage/Research Center located in Kyoto University, where he got the initial idea of this work during his stay in June 2025.
This work was supported by JSPS KAKENHI Grant Number JP24K16903.

1.7.
Notation

Throughout this paper, we fix a positive integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

For each nonnegative integers
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we set

\displaystyle[a,b]:=\{c\in\mathbb{Z}\mid a\leq c\leq b\},

\displaystyle[a]:=[1,a].

Let
\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}
denote the lexicographic order on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
:
we have
(i,j)\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}(k,l)
if and only if either
i<k
, or
i=k
and
j\leq l
.

2.
Partitions

Partitions and skew partitions naturally appear in representation theory of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
.
Besides them, partitions with “holes” often appear when we manipulate tableaux.
For rigorous treatment of such tableaux, we introduce the notion of punctured partitions.

2.1.
Partitions

A
partition
is a weakly decreasing finite sequence
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{l})
of positive integers.
The integers
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{l}
are called the
parts
of
\lambda
.
The length
l
is referred to as the
length
of
\lambda
, and denoted by
\ell(\lambda)
.
The sum
|\lambda|:=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\lambda_{i}
of parts is called the
size
of
\lambda
.

Let
\mathrm{Par}
denote the set of partitions.
Also, for each
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let
\mathrm{Par}_{\leq l}
denote the partitions of length at most
l
.

Given
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}
, it is often convenient to extend the notion of parts by setting
\lambda_{0}:=\infty
and
\lambda_{i}:=0\text{ for all }i>\ell(\lambda)
:

\lambda=(\infty,\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)},0,0,\dots)

The
Young diagram
of
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}
is the set

D(\lambda):=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2}\mid j\in[\lambda_{i}]\}.

We represent it by a collection of boxes arranged in left-justified rows with the
i
-th row from the top consisting of
\lambda_{i}
boxes.
For example, if
\lambda=(5,4,3,3,1)
, then

D(\lambda)=\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern Definition 2.1.1
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

(1)

The integer
r
is said to be an
addable row
of
\lambda
if

\lambda_{r}<\lambda_{r-1}.

In this case, we set

\operatorname{add}(\lambda,r):=(\dots,\lambda_{r-1},\lambda_{r}+1,\lambda_{r+1},\dots)\in\mathrm{Par}

(2)

The integer
r
is said to be a
removable row
of
\lambda
if

\lambda_{r}>\lambda_{r+1}.

In this case, we set

\operatorname{rm}(\lambda,r):=(\dots,\lambda_{r-1},\lambda_{r}-1,\lambda_{r+1},\dots)\in\mathrm{Par}.

Example 2.1.2
.

Let
\lambda=(5,4,3,3,1)
.
The addable rows of
\lambda
are
1,2,3,5,6
, and the removable rows are
1,2,4,5
.
We have

Lemma 2.1.3
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

(1)

If
r
is an addable row of
\lambda
, then it is a removable row of
\operatorname{add}(\lambda,r)
and we have

\operatorname{rm}(\operatorname{add}(\lambda,r),r)=\lambda.

(2)

If
r
is a removable row of
\lambda
, then it is an addable row of
\operatorname{rm}(\lambda,r)
and we have

\operatorname{add}(\operatorname{rm}(\lambda,r),r)=\lambda.

Proof.

The assertions are immediate from definitions.
∎

2.2.
Skew partitions

Define a binary relation
\subseteq
on the set
\mathrm{Par}
of partitions by declaring
\mu\subseteq\lambda
to mean that
D(\mu)\subseteq D(\lambda)
, or equivalently,

\mu_{i}\leq\lambda_{i}\quad\text{ for all }i\geq 1.

A
skew partition
is a pair
\lambda/\mu:=(\lambda,\mu)
of partitions such that
\mu\subseteq\lambda
.
The
size
of
\lambda/\mu
is the difference
|\lambda/\mu|:=|\lambda|-|\mu|
.
The
Young diagram
of
\lambda/\mu
is the set difference
D(\lambda/\mu):=D(\lambda)\setminus D(\mu)
.

Example 2.2.1
.

Let
\lambda:=(5,4,3,3,1)
and
\mu:=(4,3,1)
.
Then, we have
\mu\subset\lambda
,
|\lambda/\mu|=8
, and

D(\lambda/\mu)=\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower We often identify a partition
\lambda
with the skew partition
\lambda/()
, where
()
is the unique partition of length
0
.

Definition 2.2.2
.

Let
\lambda/\mu
be a skew partition.

(1)

We say that
\lambda/\mu
is a
horizontal strip
if its Young diagram has at most one box in each column:

\mu_{i}\in[\lambda_{i+1},\lambda_{i}]\quad\text{ for all }i\geq 1.

We write
\mu\overset{\text{hor}}{\subseteq}\lambda
to mean that
\lambda/\mu
is a horizontal strip.

(2)

We say that
\lambda/\mu
is a
vertical strip
if its Young diagram has at most one box in each row:

\mu_{i}\in\{\lambda_{i}-1,\lambda_{i}\}\quad\text{ for all }i\geq 1.

We write
\mu\overset{\text{ver}}{\subseteq}\lambda
to mean that
\lambda/\mu
is a vertical strip.

Example 2.2.3
.

Let
\lambda:=(5,4,3,3,1)
,
\mu:=(4,4,3,1,1)
,
\nu:=(5,3,2,2)
.
Then,
\lambda/\mu
is a horizontal strip, and
\lambda/\nu
is a vertical strip:

2.3.
Punctured partitions

Definition 2.3.1
.

A
punctured partition
is a pair
(\lambda,H)
consisting of a partition
\lambda
and a subset
H
of the Young diagram
D(\lambda)
of
\lambda
.
The elements of
H
are called the
holes
of
(\lambda,H)
.
The
Young diagram
of a punctured partition
(\lambda,H)
is the set difference

D(\lambda,H):=D(\lambda)\setminus H.

We represent the Young diagram of a punctured partition
(\lambda,H)
by filling in each box of
D(\lambda)
corresponding to a hole with a circle.
When we do not care whether a box in
D(\lambda)
is a hole or not, we paint it gray.

Example 2.3.2
.

Let
\lambda=(5,4,3,3,1)
and
H:=\{(1,2),(1,3),(2,4),(3,1),(4,1),(4,2)\}
.
Then,
(\lambda,H)
is a punctured partition, and we have

0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\bigcirc$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\bigcirc$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}.

Remark 2.3.3
.

Let
\lambda/\mu
be a skew partition.
Then,
(\lambda,D(\mu))
is a punctured partition.
We often identify them.
In particular,
\lambda=\lambda/()
is a punctured partition without holes.

Definition 2.3.4
.

Let
(\lambda,H)
be a punctured partition, and
(r,c)\in H
.

(1)

We say that
(r,c)
is
slidable
if either
(r,c+1)
or
(r+1,c)
is a member of
D(\lambda,H)
.

(2)

We say that
(r,c)
is
reversely slidable
if either
(r,c-1)
or
(r-1,c)
is a member of
D(\lambda,H)
.

Example 2.3.5
.

Let
(\lambda,H)
be as in Example
2.3.2
.
Then, the slidable holes are
(1,2)
,
(1,3)
,
(3,1)
,
(4,1)
,
(4,2)
, while the reversely slidable holes are
(1,2)
,
(2,4)
,
(3,1)
,
(4,2)
.

Lemma 2.3.6
.

Let
(\lambda,H)
be a punctured partition, and
(r,c)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
.

(1)

Suppose that there is no slidable hole of the form
(r,c^{\prime})
with
c^{\prime}>c
, and set

q:=\min\{j\geq 0\mid(r,c+j+1)\notin D(\lambda,H)\}.

Then, we have

\{j>c\mid(r,j)\in D(\lambda,H)\}=[c+1,c+q];

q
\lambda_{r}
c
r

(2)

Suppose that there is no reversely slidable hole of the form
(r,c^{\prime})
with
c^{\prime}<c
, and set

p:=\min\{j\geq 0\mid(r,c-j-1)\notin D(\lambda,H)\}.

Then, we have

\{j<c\mid(r,j)\in D(\lambda,H)\}=[c-p,c-1];

p
\lambda_{r}
c
r

Proof.

We prove only the first assertion; the second one can be proved similarly.
By the definition of
q
, we have

(r,j)\in D(\lambda,H)\quad\text{ for all }j\in[c+1,c+q].

Hence, we only need to show that
(r,j)\notin D(\lambda,H)
for all
j>c+q
.
Assume contrary that
(r,j)\in D(\lambda,H)
for some
j>c+q
.
We may further assume that this
j
is the minimum among those.
Since
(r,c+q+1)\notin D(\lambda,H)
by the definition of
q
, we must have
j>c+q+1
and
(r,j-1)\notin D(\lambda,H)
.
This implies that
(r,j-1)
is a slidable hole.
However, this contradicts our assumption on
(r,c)
.
Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

Proposition 2.3.7
.

Let
(\lambda,H)
be a punctured partition.

(1)

If
(\lambda,H)
has no slidable holes, then there exists a partition
\mu\subseteq\lambda
such that
D(\lambda,H)=D(\mu)
.

(2)

If
(\lambda,H)
has no reversely slidable holes, then there exists a partition
\nu\subseteq\lambda
such that
D(\lambda,H)=D(\lambda/\nu)
.

Proof.

Let us prove the first assertion.
For each
i\geq 1
, set

\mu_{i}:=\min\{j\geq 0\mid(i,j+1)\notin D(\lambda,H)\}.

By Lemma
2.3.6
(
1
), we have

D(\lambda,H)=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2}\mid j\in[\mu_{i}]\}.

It remains to show that
\mu:=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots)
is a partition contained in
\lambda
.
By the definition of
\mu_{i}
’s, we see that
\mu_{i}\leq\lambda_{i}
for all
i\geq 1
.
In particular,
\mu_{i}=0
for all
i>\ell(\lambda)
.
Hence, we only need to prove that
\mu_{i}\geq\mu_{i+1}
for all
i\geq 1
.
Assume contrary that
\mu_{i}<\mu_{i+1}
for some
i\geq 1
.
Then, we have
(i,\mu_{i+1})\notin D(\lambda,H)
and
(i+1,\mu_{i+1})\in D(\lambda,H)
.
Since
\mu_{i+1}\leq\lambda_{i+1}\leq\lambda_{i}
, these imply that
(i,\mu_{i+1})
is a slidable hole.
However, this contradicts our assumption that
(\lambda,H)
has no slidable holes.
Thus, we complete the proof of the first assertion.

The second assertion can be prove in a similar way to the first one; we use Lemma
2.3.6
(
2
) instead of (
1
) in this case.
∎

Remark 2.3.8
.

Based on Proposition
2.3.7
, we often regard punctured partitions without slidable holes as partitions, and those without reversely slidable holes as skew partitions.

3.
Semistandard tableaux

In this section, we study various algorithms involving semistandard tableaux such as the Schensted row-insertion, the Schützenberger sliding, and the rectification.
They are used to define the Berele row-insertion and the row-insertion of type
A\mathrm{II}
in the subsequent sections.

Throughout this section, we fix a punctured partition
(\lambda,H)
.

3.1.
Semistandard punctured tableaux

Definition 3.1.1
.

A
punctured tableau
of shape
(\lambda,H)
with entries in
[n]
is a map

T:D(\lambda,H)\to[n].

Let
\operatorname{sh}(T):=(\lambda,H)
denote the shape of
T
.
The values
T(i,j)
with
(i,j)\in D(\lambda,H)
are called the
entries
of
T
.

Given a punctured tableau
T
of shape
(\lambda,H)
, we set

T(i,j):=\infty\quad\text{ for each }(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2}\setminus D(\lambda,H).

A tableau
T
of shape
(\lambda,H)
is represented by filling in the boxes of the Young diagram
D(\lambda,H)
with their entries.

Example 3.1.2
.

Let
(\lambda,H)
as in Example
2.3.2
.
The following is a tableau of shape
(\lambda,H)
.

2

4

3

\bigcirc

\bigcirc

8

\bigcirc

9

5

Remark 3.1.3
.

The notion of punctured tableaux has already appeared in
[
Ber86
]
, but in a more restricted meaning.
Namely, the punctured tableaux there are always supposed to have exactly one hole;
|H|=1
.

When we regard a punctured partition
(\lambda,H)
without slidable holes as a partition
\mu
as in Remark
2.3.8
, we say that a punctured tableau
T
of shape
(\lambda,H)
is a
tableau
of shape
\mu
and write
\operatorname{sh}(T)=\mu
.
Similarly, when we regard a punctured partition
(\lambda,H)
without reversely slidable holes as a skew partition
\lambda/\nu
, we say that a punctured tableau
T
of shape
(\lambda,H)
is a
skew tableau
of shape
\lambda/\nu
and write
\operatorname{sh}(T)=\lambda/\nu
.

Definition 3.1.4
.

A punctured tableau
T
of shape
(\lambda,H)
with entries in
[n]
is said to be
semistandard
if its entries weakly increase along the rows from the left to right and strictly increase along the columns from the top to bottom: for each
(i,j),(k,l)\in D(\lambda,H)
, it holds that

(1)

T(i,j)\leq T(k,l)
if
i=k
and
j<l
,

(2)

T(i,j)<T(k,l)
if
i<k
and
j=l
.

Example 3.1.5
.

The punctured tableau in Example
3.1.2
is semistandard.

Let
\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda,H)
denote the set of semistandard punctured tableaux of shape
(\lambda,H)
with entries in
[n]
.
Similarly, let
\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\mu)
denote the set of semistandard tableaux of shape
\mu
with entries in
[n]
, and
\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda/\nu)
the set of semistandard skew tableaux of shape
\lambda/\nu
with entries in
[n]
.

3.2.
Words

Let
\mathcal{W}_{n}
denote the free monoid of words with letters in
[n]
:

\mathcal{W}_{n}:=\bigsqcup_{l\geq 0}\,[n]^{l}.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Two words
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{n}
are said to be
Knuth equivalent
if they can be transformed into each other by a sequence of the elementary Knuth transformations

(K1):

yzx\mapsto yxz
if
x<y\leq z
,

(K2):

xzy\mapsto zxy
if
x\leq y<z
,

and their inverses.
In this case, we write
w\equiv w^{\prime}
.

Definition 3.2.2
.

Let
T
be a punctured tableau of shape
(\lambda,H)
.

(1)

The
row-word
of
T
is the word
w_{\mathrm{row}}(T)
obtained by reading the entries of
T
along the rows from the left to right and from the bottom to top.

(2)

The
column-word
of
T
is the word
w_{\mathrm{col}}(T)
obtained by reading the entries of
T
along the columns from the bottom to top and from the left to right.

Example 3.2.3
.

Let
T
denote the punctured tableau in Example
3.1.2
.
Then, we have

w_{\mathrm{row}}(T)=(5,9,6,8,3,4,4,2,2,4),\quad w_{\mathrm{col}}(T)=(5,3,2,6,4,9,8,4,2,4).

Regarding the Knuth equivalence, let us recall the following results for later use.

Proposition 3.2.4

(
cf
.
[
Ful97
, §2.1 Theorem]
)
.

Let
T,S
be semistandard tableaux.
If
w_{\mathrm{row}}(T)\equiv w_{\mathrm{row}}(S)
, then we have
T=S
.

Proposition 3.2.5

(
[
Ful97
, §2.3 equation (10)]
)
.

Let
T
be a semistandard skew tableau.
Then, we have

w_{\mathrm{row}}(T)\equiv w_{\mathrm{col}}(T).

Lemma 3.2.6

(
[
Ful97
, the proof of §2.1 Proposition 2]
)
.

Let
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
u_{1},\dots,u_{p}
,
v_{1},\dots,v_{p}
,
x
,
y_{1},\dots,y_{q}
,
z_{1},\dots,z_{q}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
Assume the following
:

•

u_{1}\leq\cdots\leq u_{p}
,

•

v_{1}\leq\cdots\leq v_{p}
,

•

u_{i}<v_{i}
for all
i\in[p]
,

•

v_{p}\leq x\leq y_{1}
,

•

y_{1}\leq\cdots\leq y_{q}
,

•

z_{1}\leq\cdots\leq z_{q}
,

•

y_{j}<z_{j}
for all
j\in[q]
.

Then, we have

(1)

\displaystyle v_{1}\cdots v_{p}xz_{1}\cdots z_{q}u_{1}\cdots u_{p}y_{1}\cdots y_{q}\equiv v_{1}\cdots v_{p}z_{1}\cdots z_{q}u_{1}\cdots u_{p}xy_{1}\cdots y_{q}.

Remark 3.2.7
.

The left-hand side of equation (
1
) in Lemma
3.2.6
equals the row-word of the punctured tableau

This observation would help us to relate the Knuth equivalence and the sliding algorithm.

3.3.
Row-insertion

Definition 3.3.1
.

Let
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda)
,
r
an addable row of
\lambda
, and
x\in[n]
.
Define a tableau
\operatorname{add}(T,r,x)
of shape
\operatorname{add}(\lambda,r)
by

(\operatorname{add}(T,r,x))(i,j):=\begin{cases}x&\text{ if }(i,j)=(r,\lambda_{r}+1),\\
T(i,j)&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

for each
(i,j)\in D(\operatorname{add}(\lambda,r))
.

Definition 3.3.2
.

The
row-insertion
is the algorithm which takes
(T,x)\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda)\times[n]
as an input, and outputs a new semistandard tableau
T\leftarrow x
as follows.

(1)

Initialize
r:=1
,
T_{r}:=T
,
x_{r}:=x
.

(2)

Set
c_{r}:=\min\{j\geq 1\mid T(r,j)>x_{r}\}
.

(3)

(a)

If
c_{r}=\lambda_{r}+1
, then return
\operatorname{add}(T,r,x)
.

(b)

Otherwise, set
T_{r+1}
to be the tableau of shape
\lambda
obtained from
T_{r}
by replacing the
(r,c_{r})
-entry with
x_{r}
:

T_{r+1}(i,j):=\begin{cases}x_{r}&\text{ if }(i,j)=(r,c_{r}),\\
T_{r}(i,j)&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

for each
(i,j)\in D(\lambda)
.
Also, set
x_{r+1}:=T_{r}(r,c_{r})
, increment
r
, and then go back to step (
2
).

As a byproduct of this algorithm, we obtain the sequences
(c_{1},\dots,c_{r})
and
(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})
.
We call them the
row-insertion route
and the
row-inserting letters
for
(T,x)
, respectively.

0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$8$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$9$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$8$\hss}\vss}}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$9$\hss}\vss}}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt},

where the shaded boxes represent the row-insertion route and the row-inserting letters; they are
(4,3,1,1,1,1)
and
(2,3,4,6,8,9)
, respectively.

Proposition 3.3.4
.

Let
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda)
and
x\in[n]
.
Set
S:=T\leftarrow x
,
\mu:=\operatorname{sh}(S)
, and let
(c_{1},\dots,c_{r})
and
(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})
denote the row-insertion route and the row-inserting letters for
(T,x)
, respectively.
Then, the following hold:

(1)

r\in[\ell(\lambda)+1]
,

(2)

c_{i}=\min\{j\geq 1\mid T(i,j)>x_{i}\}
for all
i\in[r]
,

(3)

c_{1}\geq\cdots\geq c_{r}=\lambda_{r}+1=\mu_{r}
,

(4)

x_{1}=x
and
x_{i}=T(i-1,c_{i-1})
for all
i\in[2,r]
,

(5)

x_{1}<\cdots<x_{r}
,

(6)

\mu=\operatorname{add}(\lambda,r)
,

(7)

for each
(i,j)\in D(\mu)
, we have

S(i,j)=\begin{cases}x_{i}&\text{ if }i\in[r]\text{ and }j=c_{i},\\
T(i,j)&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

The assertions are straightforwardly deduced from Definition
3.3.2
.
∎

Theorem 3.3.5

(
[
Ful97
, §1.1]
)
.

The row-insertion gives rise to a bijection

\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda)\times[n]\to\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}\\
\mu\supset\lambda,\ |\mu/\lambda|=1\end{subarray}}\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\mu).

3.4.
Sliding and the rectification

Definition 3.4.1

(
cf
.
[
Ful97
, §1.2]
)
.

The
sliding
is the algorithm which takes a pair
(T,(r,c))
consisting of a semistandard punctured tableau
T
of shape
(\lambda,H)
and a hole
(r,c)\in H
as input, and outputs a new semistandard punctured tableau
\operatorname{Sl}(T;r,c)
as follows.

(1)

Initialize
t:=0
,
T_{t}:=T
,
r_{t}:=r
,
c_{t}:=c
,
H_{t}:=H
.

(2)

(a)

If
(r_{t},c_{t})
is not slidable, then return
T_{t}
.

(b)

If
T(r_{t},c_{t}+1)<T(r_{t}+1,c_{t})
, then set
r_{t+1}:=r_{t}
,
c_{t+1}:=c_{t}+1
.

(c)

If
T(r_{t},c_{t}+1)\geq T(r_{t}+1,c_{t})
, then set
r_{t+1}:=r_{t}+1
,
c_{t+1}:=c_{t}
.

(3)

Set
H_{t+1}:=(H_{t}\setminus\{(r_{t},c_{t})\})\sqcup\{(r_{t+1},c_{t+1})\}
, and
T_{t+1}
to be the semistandard punctured tableau of shape
(\lambda,H_{t+1})
defined by

T_{t+1}(i,j):=\begin{cases}T_{t}(r_{t+1},c_{t+1})&\text{ if }(i,j)=(r_{t},c_{t}),\\
T_{t}(i,j)&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

for each
(i,j)\in D(\lambda,H_{t+1})
.

(4)

Increment
t
and then go back to step (
2
).

.

where the shaded boxes represent the route of the hole.

For a later use, we describe another algorithm which computes the sliding.

Proposition 3.4.3
.

Let
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda,H)
and
(r,c)\in H
.
Let
S
denote the punctured tableau obtained by the following algorithm.

(1)

Initialize
k:=r-1
,
T_{k}:=T
,
H_{k}:=H
,
\gamma_{k}:=c
.

(2)

Set

\displaystyle\gamma_{k+1}:=\min\{j\geq\gamma_{k}\mid T_{k}(k+2,j)\leq T_{k}(k+1,j+1)\},

\displaystyle H^{\prime}_{k+1}:=(H_{k}\setminus\{(k+1,\gamma_{k})\})\sqcup\{(k+1,\gamma_{k+1})\},

and
T^{\prime}_{k+1}
to be the punctured tableau of shape
(\lambda,H^{\prime}_{k+1})
defined by

T^{\prime}_{k+1}(i,j):=\begin{cases}T_{k}(k+1,j+1)&\text{ if }i=k+1\text{ and }j\in[\gamma_{k},\gamma_{k+1}-1],\\
T_{k}(i,j)&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

for each
(i,j)\in D(\lambda,H^{\prime}_{k+1})
.

(3)

(a)

If
(k+2,\gamma_{k+1})\notin D(\lambda,H^{\prime}_{k+1})
, then return
T^{\prime}_{k+1}
.

(b)

Otherwise, set

H_{k+1}:=(H^{\prime}_{k+1}\setminus\{(k+1,\gamma_{k+1})\})\sqcup\{(k+2,\gamma_{k+1})\},

T_{k+1}
to be the punctured tableau of shape
(\lambda,H_{k+1})
defined by

T_{k+1}(i,j):=\begin{cases}T^{\prime}_{k+1}(k+2,\gamma_{k+1})&\text{ if }(i,j)=(k+1,\gamma_{k+1}),\\
T^{\prime}_{k+1}(i,j)&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

for each
(i,j)\in D(\lambda,H_{k+1})
.
Increment
k
and then go back to step
(
2
)
.

Then, we have

S=\operatorname{Sl}(T;r,c).

In particular, there exists a sequence
(\gamma_{r-1},\gamma_{r},\dots,\gamma_{k})
of positive integers satisfying the following:

(1)

c=\gamma_{r-1}\leq\cdots\leq\gamma_{k}
,

(2)

\gamma_{i}:=\min\{j\geq\gamma_{i-1}\mid T(i+1,j)\leq T(i,j+1)\}
for all
i\in[r,k]
,

(3)

\operatorname{sh}(\operatorname{Sl}(T;r,c))=(\lambda,H^{\prime})
, where
H^{\prime}:=(H\setminus\{(r,c)\})\sqcup\{(k,\gamma_{k})\})
,

(4)

(k,\gamma_{k})
is not a slidable hole of
(\lambda,H^{\prime})
.

(5)

for each
(i,j)\in D(\lambda,H^{\prime})
, we have

(\operatorname{Sl}(T;r,c))(i,j)=\begin{cases}T(i,j+1)&\text{ if }i\in[r,k]\text{ and }j\in[\gamma_{i-1},\gamma_{i}-1],\\
T(i+1,j)&\text{ if }i\in[r,k-1]\text{ and }j=\gamma_{i},\\
T(i,j)&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

The assertions are straightforwardly deduced from Definition
3.4.1
.
∎

Definition 3.4.4
.

We call the sequence
(\gamma_{r-1},\gamma_{r},\dots,\gamma_{k})
in Proposition
3.4.3
the
sliding route
for
(T;r,c)
.
Also, we call
k
the
terminal row
of the sliding for
(T;r,c)
.

Example 3.4.5
.

Let
T
be as in Exercise
3.4.2
.
Then, the sliding route for
(T;2,1)
is
(1,3,3,3)
, and the terminal row is
4
.

Clearly, the sliding changes the row-word of a given tableau in general.
However, it does not change the Knuth equivalence class of the row-word when the given hole is the latest slidable hole with respect to the lexicographic order
\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}
, as we will see below.

Lemma 3.4.6
.

Assume that
(\lambda,H)
has a slidable hole, and that the latest slidable hole
(r,c)
with respect to
\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}
satisfies
(r+1,c)\in D(\lambda,H)
.
Set

•

p:=c-1
,

•

q:=\min\{j\geq 0\mid(r+1,c+j+1)\notin D(\lambda,H)\}
,

•

p^{\prime}:=\sharp\{j\in[c-1]\mid(r,j)\in D(\lambda,H)\}
,

•

q^{\prime}:=\min\{j\geq 0\mid(r,c+j+1)\notin D(\lambda,H)\}
.

Then, the following hold:

(1)

p\geq p^{\prime}
,

(2)

q\leq q^{\prime}
,

(3)

\{j\geq 1\mid(r+1,j)\in D(\lambda,H)\}=[p+q+1]
,

(4)

\{j>c\mid(r,j)\in D(\lambda,H)\}=[c+1,c+q^{\prime}]
.

Namely, the
r
-th and
(r+1)
-st rows of
D(\lambda,H)
is of the following form:

p^{\prime}
boxes
q^{\prime}
p
q
c
r
r+1

Proof.

By the definitions of
p
and
p^{\prime}
, we have

p\geq p^{\prime}.

The other assertions follow from Lemma
2.3.6
(
1
) and a similar argument to the proof of Proposition
2.3.7
(
1
).
∎

Proposition 3.4.7
.

Let
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda,H)
and
(r,c)\in H
.
If
(r,c)
is the latest slidable hole with respect to
\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}
, then we have

w_{\mathrm{row}}(T)\equiv w_{\mathrm{row}}(\operatorname{Sl}(T;r,c)).

Proof.

Let us keep the notation in Definition
3.4.1
.
We will prove by induction on
t\geq 0
the following:
if
(r_{t},c_{t})
is the latest slidable hole of
(\lambda,H_{t})
, then the following hold.

(1)

w_{\mathrm{row}}(T_{t})\equiv w_{\mathrm{row}}(T_{t+1})
.

(2)

If
(r_{t+1},c_{t+1})
is a slidable hole of
(\lambda,H_{t+1})
, then it is the latest slidable hole.

Since
(r_{t+1},c_{t+1})
equals either
(r_{t},c_{t}+1)
or
(r_{t}+1,c_{t})
, we have

(r_{t},c_{t})\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}(r_{t+1},c_{t+1}).

Also, since

H_{t+1}=(H_{t}\setminus\{(r_{t},c_{t})\})\sqcup\{(r_{t+1},c_{t+1})\},

each slidable hole
(k,l)
of
(\lambda,H_{t+1})
must satisfy one of the following conditions:

•

(k,l)=(r_{t+1},c_{t+1})
,

•

(k,l)
is a slidable hole of
(\lambda,H_{t})
,

•

(k,l)=\{(r_{t},c_{t}-1),\ (r_{t}-1,c_{t})\}
.

Hence, claim (
2
) follows.

Let us prove the claim (
1
).
If
(r_{t+1},c_{t+1})=(r_{t},c_{t}+1)
, then
T_{t}
and
T_{t+1}
have the same row-word.
In this case, we have nothing to prove.
Hence, assume that
(r_{t+1},c_{t+1})=(r_{t}+1,c_{t})
.
By Lemma
3.4.6
, we see that

w_{\mathrm{row}}(T_{t})=w_{1}v_{p-p^{\prime}+1}\cdots v_{p}xz_{1}\cdots z_{q}u_{1}\cdots u_{p^{\prime}}y_{1}\cdots y_{q}w_{2},

where

•

w_{1},w_{2}
are some words,

•

p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime}
are some nonnegative integers such that
p=c_{t}-1
,
p\geq p^{\prime}
and
q\leq q^{\prime}
,

•

v_{j}=T_{t}(r_{t}+1,j)
for all
j\in[p-p^{\prime}+1,p]
,

•

x=T_{t}(r_{t}+1,c_{t})
,

•

z_{j}=T_{t}(r_{t}+1,c_{t}+j)
for all
j\in[q]
,

•

u_{j}=T_{t}(r_{t},l_{j})
for all
j\in[p^{\prime}]
, where
1\leq l_{1}<\cdots<l_{p^{\prime}}<c_{t}
are such that

\{j<c_{t}\mid(r_{t},j)\in D(\lambda,H)\}=\{l_{1},\dots,l_{p^{\prime}}\},

•

y_{j}=T_{t}(r_{t},c_{t}+j)
for all
j\in[q]
.

Then, we can apply Lemma
3.2.6
to obtain

w_{\mathrm{row}}(T_{t})\equiv w_{1}v_{p-p^{\prime}+1}\cdots v_{p}z_{1}\cdots z_{q}u_{1}\cdots u_{p^{\prime}}xy_{1}\cdots y_{q}w_{2}.

The right-hand side is nothing but
w_{\mathrm{row}}(T_{t+1})
; see Remark
3.2.7
.
Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

Definition 3.4.8
.

The
rectification
of
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda,H)
is the semistandard tableau
\operatorname{Rect}(T)
obtained by the following algorithm.

(1)

Initialize
t:=0
,
T_{t}:=T
,
H_{t}:=H
.

(2)

(a)

If
H_{t}
has no slidable holes, then return
T_{t}
.

(b)

Otherwise, let
(r,c)\in H_{t}
denote the latest slidable hole with respect to
\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}
, and set
T_{t+1}:=\operatorname{Sl}(T_{t};r,c)
.

(3)

Increment
t
and then go back to step (
2
).

.

Proposition 3.4.10
.

Let
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda,H)
.
Then, we have

w_{\mathrm{row}}(T)\equiv w_{\mathrm{row}}(\operatorname{Rect}(T)).

Proof.

The assertion immediately follows from Proposition
3.4.7
.
∎

Proposition
3.4.10
, together with Proposition
3.2.4
, implies that
\operatorname{Rect}(T)
is the unique semistandard tableau whose row-word is Knuth equivalent to
w_{\mathrm{row}}(T)
.
This is a slight generalization of
[
Ful97
, §2.1 Corollary 1]
.

3.5.
Reverse sliding and the reverse rectification

Almost all the results in this subsection have a counterpart in the previous subsection, and their proofs are similar.
Hence, we omit such proofs here.

Definition 3.5.1
.

The
reverse sliding
is the algorithm which takes a pair
(T,(r,c))\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda,H)\times H
as an input, and outputs a new semistandard punctured tableau
\operatorname{Sl}(T;r,c)
as follows.

(1)

Initialize
t:=0
,
T_{t}:=T
,
r_{t}:=r
,
c_{t}:=c
,
H_{t}:=H
.

(2)

(a)

If
(r_{t},c_{t})
is not reversely slidable, then return
T_{t}
.

(b)

If
T(r_{t},c_{t}-1)>T(r_{t}-1,c_{t})
, then set
r_{t+1}:=r_{t}
,
c_{t+1}:=c_{t}-1
.

(c)

If
T(r_{t},c_{t}-1)\leq T(r_{t}-1,c_{t})
, then set
r_{t+1}:=r_{t}-1
,
c_{t+1}:=c_{t}
.

(3)

Set
H_{t+1}:=(H_{t}\setminus\{(r_{t},c_{t})\})\sqcup\{(r_{t+1},c_{t+1})\}
, and
T_{t+1}
to be the semistandard punctured tableau of shape
(\lambda,H_{t+1})
defined by

T_{t+1}(i,j):=\begin{cases}T_{t}(r_{t+1},c_{t+1})&\text{ if }(i,j)=(r_{t},c_{t}),\\
T_{t}(i,j)&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

for each
(i,j)\in D(\lambda,H_{t+1})
.

(4)

Increment
t
and then go back to step (
2
).

Example 3.5.2
.

Let

T:=\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern where the shaded boxes represent the route of the hole.

Lemma 3.5.3
.

Assume that
H
has a reversely slidable hole, and that the first reversely slidable hole
(r,c)
with respect to
\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}
satisfies
(r-1,c)\in D(\lambda,H)
.
Set

•

p:=\min\{j\geq 0\mid(r-1,c-j-1)\notin D(\lambda,H)\}
,

•

q:=\lambda_{r-1}-c
,

•

p^{\prime}:=\min\{j\geq 0\mid(r,c-j-1)\notin D(\lambda,H)\}
.

•

q^{\prime}:=\sharp\{j>c\mid(r,j)\in D(\lambda,H)\}
,

Then, the following hold:

(1)

p\leq p^{\prime}
,

(2)

q\geq q^{\prime}
,

(3)

\{j\geq 1\mid(r-1,j)\in D(\lambda,H)\}=[c-p,c+q]
,

(4)

\{j<c\mid(r,j)\in D(\lambda,H)\}=[c-p^{\prime},c-1]
.

Namely, the
(r-1)
-st and
r
-th rows of
D(\lambda,H)
is of the following form:

p
q
p^{\prime}
q^{\prime}
boxes
c
r-1
r

Proposition 3.5.4
.

Let
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda,H)
and
(r,c)\in H
.
If
(r,c)
is the first reversely slidable hole with respect to
\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}
, then we have

w_{\mathrm{row}}(T)\equiv w_{\mathrm{row}}(\operatorname{revSl}(T;r,c)).

Definition 3.5.5
.

The
reverse rectification
of
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda,H)
is the semistandard skew tableau
\operatorname{revRect}(T)
obtained by the following algorithm.

(1)

Initialize
t:=0
,
T_{t}:=T
,
H_{t}:=H
.

(2)

(a)

If
H_{t}
has no reversely slidable holes, then return
T_{t}
.

(b)

Otherwise, let
(r,c)\in H_{t}
denote the first reversely slidable hole with respect to
\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}
, and set
T_{t+1}:=\operatorname{revSl}(T_{t};r,c)
.

(3)

Increment
t
and then go back to step (
2
).

\operatorname{revRect}(T)=\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$8$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$9$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$8$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$9$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}.

Proposition 3.5.7
.

Let
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda,H)
.
Then, we have

w_{\mathrm{row}}(T)\equiv w_{\mathrm{row}}(\operatorname{revRect}(T)).

Corollary 3.5.8
.

Let
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda,H)
.
Then, we have

\operatorname{Rect}(T)=\operatorname{Rect}(\operatorname{revRect}(T));

here we regard
\operatorname{revRect}(T)
as a punctured tableau by identifying its shape, which is a skew partition, as a punctured partition
; see Remark
2.3.3
.

Proof.

By Propositions
3.4.10
and
3.5.7
, we have

w_{\mathrm{row}}(\operatorname{Rect}(T))\equiv w_{\mathrm{row}}(T)\equiv w_{\mathrm{row}}(\operatorname{revRect}(T))\equiv w_{\mathrm{row}}(\operatorname{Rect}(\operatorname{revRect}(T))).

Then, Proposition
3.2.4
implies the assertion.
∎

Corollary 3.5.9
.

Assume that the holes of
(\lambda,H)
lie in the first column:

H\subset\{(r,1)\mid r\geq 1\}.

Then, for each
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{n}(\lambda,H)
, we have

w_{\mathrm{row}}(\operatorname{Rect}(T))\equiv w_{\mathrm{col}}(T).

Proof.

By Corollary
3.5.8
and Proposition
3.4.10
, we have

w_{\mathrm{row}}(\operatorname{Rect}(T))=w_{\mathrm{row}}(\operatorname{Rect}(\operatorname{revRect}(T)))\equiv w_{\mathrm{row}}(\operatorname{revRect}(T)).

Then, Proposition
3.2.5
implies that

w_{\mathrm{row}}(\operatorname{revRect}(T))\equiv w_{\mathrm{col}}(\operatorname{revRect}(T)).

By our assumption, it is clear that
w_{\mathrm{col}}(T)=w_{\mathrm{col}}(\operatorname{revRect}(T))
.
Therefore, the assertion follows.
∎

4.
Berele row-insertion

In this section, we study the Berele row-insertion in detail.
The original definition of the Berele row-insertion involves the Schensted row-insertion and sliding.
We rewrite it in terms of the Knuth equivalence.

Throughout this section, we fix a partition
\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
.

4.1.
Symplectic tableaux

Definition 4.1.1

(
[
Kin76
, §4]
)
.

A semistandard tableau
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\nu)
is said to be
symplectic
if

T(i,1)\geq 2i-1\quad\text{ for all }i\geq 1.

Let
Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)
denote the set of symplectic tableaux in
\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\nu)
.

Lemma 4.1.2
.

Let
T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)
and
x\in[2n]
.
Set
S:=T\leftarrow x
.
Let
(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})
denote the row-inserting letters for
(T,x)
.
Then, the following hold:

(1)

If
x_{i}\geq 2i-1
for all
i\in[r]
, then
S
is symplectic.

(2)

If
x_{i}<2i-1
for some
i\in[r]
, then
S
is not symplectic.
Moreover, if
s
denotes the minimum among those
i
, then the following hold:

(a)

s\geq 2
,

(b)

x_{s-1}=2s-3
,

(c)

x_{s}=2s-2
,

(d)

x_{i}=T(i-1,1)
for all
i\in[s+1,r]
.

Proof.

Let us prove the first assertion.
By Proposition
3.3.4
(
7
), we have

S(i,1)\in\{x_{i},T(i,1)\}\quad\text{ for all }i\in[r],

and

S(i,1)=T(i,1)\quad\text{ for all }i>r.

Since
T
is symplectic, we have
T(i,1)\geq 2i-1
for all
i\geq 1
.
Hence, we see that

S(i,1)\geq 2i-1\quad\text{ for all }i\geq 1.

This implies that
S
is symplectic.

Let us prove the second assertion.
Let
(c_{1},\dots,c_{r})
denote the row-insertion route for
(T,x)
.
Since
x_{s}\geq 1=2\cdot 1-1
, we obtain
s\geq 2
.
By the definition of
s
and Proposition
3.3.4
(
5
), we obtain

2s-1>x_{s}>x_{s-1}\geq 2(s-1)-1=2s-3.

This implies that

x_{s-1}=2s-3,\quad x_{s}=2s-2.

By Proposition
3.3.4
(
2
), we have

c_{s}=\min\{j\geq 1\mid T(s,j)>x_{s}\}.

Since
T
is symplectic, it holds that
T(s,1)\geq 2s-1>x_{s}
.
Hence, we obtain
c_{s}=1
.
Then, Proposition
3.3.4
(
3
) implies that
c_{i}=1
for all
i\in[s,r]
.
By Proposition
3.3.4
(
4
), we obtain

x_{i}=T(i-1,c_{i-1})=T(i-1,1)\quad\text{ for all }i\in[s+1,r].

Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

4.2.
Berele row-insertion

Definition 4.2.1

(
[
Ber86
, §2]
)
.

The
Berele row-insertion
is the algorithm which takes a pair
(T,x)\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)\times[2n]
as input, and outputs a new symplectic tableau
T\xleftarrow{\mathrm{B}}x
as follows.

(1)

Initialize
r:=1
,
T_{r}:=T
,
x_{r}:=x
.

(2)

Set
c_{r}:=\min\{j\geq 1\mid T(r,j)>x_{r}\}
.

(3)

(a)

If
c_{r}=\nu_{r}+1
, then return
\operatorname{add}(T_{r},r,x_{r})
.

(b)

If
c_{r}\leq\nu_{r}
,
x_{r}=2r-1
, and
T(r,c_{r})=2r
, then set
S
to be the semistandard punctured tableau of shape
(\nu,\{(r,1)\})
defined by

S(i,j):=\begin{cases}2r-1&\text{ if }(i,j)=(r,c_{r}),\\
T_{r}(i,j)&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

for each
(i,j)\in D(\nu,\{(r,1)\})
.
Then, return
\operatorname{Rect}(S)
.

(c)

Otherwise, set
T_{r+1}
to be the symplectic tableau of shape
\lambda
obtained from
T_{r}
by replacing the
(r,c_{r})
-entry with
x_{r}
:

T_{r+1}(i,j):=\begin{cases}x_{r}&\text{ if }(i,j)=(r,c_{r}),\\
T_{r}(i,j)&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

for each
(i,j)\in D(\nu)
.
Also, set
x_{r+1}:=T_{r}(r,c_{r})
, increment
r
, and then go back to step (
2
).

Theorem 4.2.2

(
cf
.
[
Ber86
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
.
The Berele row-insertion gives rise to a bijection

Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)\times[2n]\to\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}\\
\xi\supset\nu,\ |\xi/\nu|=1\end{subarray}}Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\xi)\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}\\
\xi\subset\nu,\ |\nu/\xi|=1\end{subarray}}Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\xi).

Lemma 4.2.3
.

Let
T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)
and
x\in[2n]
.
Let
(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})
denote the row-inserting letters for
(T,x)
.
Set
S:=T\xleftarrow{\mathrm{B}}x
and
\xi:=\operatorname{sh}(S)
.

(1)

Suppose that
x_{i}\geq 2i-1
for all
i\in[r]
.
Then, we have

(a)

S=T\leftarrow x
,

(b)

\xi=\operatorname{add}(\nu,r)
.

(2)

Suppose that
x_{i}<2i-1
for some
i\in[r]
.
Let
s
denote the minimum among those
i
, and set
T^{\prime}
to be the punctured tableau of shape
(\nu,\{(s-1,1)\})
defined by

T^{\prime}(i,j):=\begin{cases}x_{i}&\text{ if }i\in[s-1]\text{ and }j=c_{i},\\
T(i,j)&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

for each
(i,j)\in D(\nu,\{(s-1,1)\})
.
Then, we have

(a)

S=\operatorname{Rect}(T^{\prime})
,

(b)

\xi=\operatorname{rm}(\nu,k)
, where
k
denotes the terminal row of the sliding for
(T^{\prime};s-1,1)
.

Proof.

The assertion follows from Definition
4.2.1
, Proposition
3.3.4
(
6
), and Lemma
4.1.2
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{R}:=\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\sqcup\{\bar{r}\mid r\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\}
denote the totally ordered set such that

1<2<\cdots<\bar{2}<\bar{1}.

An element of
\mathcal{R}
is said to be
unbarred
if it belongs to
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and
barred
otherwise.

Definition 4.2.4
.

Let us keep the notation in Lemma
4.2.3
.
The
terminal row
of the Berele row-insertion for
(T,x)
is
r\in\mathcal{R}
if
x_{i}\geq 2i-1
for all
i\in[r]
; otherwise
\bar{k}\in\mathcal{R}
.

Definition 4.2.5
.

Let us keep the notation in Lemma
4.2.3
.
Suppose that
x_{i}<2i-1
for some
i\in[r]
, and let
(\gamma_{s-2},\gamma_{s-1},\dots,\gamma_{k})
denote the sliding route for
(T^{\prime};s-1,1)
.
We call
(c_{1},\dots,c_{s-1};\gamma_{s-1},\dots,\gamma_{k})
the Berele row-insertion route for
(T,x)
.

Example 4.2.6
.

Let

T:=\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$6$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern Then,

T\xleftarrow{\mathrm{B}}2=\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$8$\hss}\vss}}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$8$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$6$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$9$\hss}\vss}}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$8$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$8$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\bigcirc$\hss}\vss}}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$9$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt},

where the shaded boxes represent the Berele row-insertion route
(4,3;3,3,3)
.
The terminal row is
\bar{4}
.

Lemma 4.2.7

(Berele row-insertion lemma)
.

Let
T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)
,
x,x^{\prime}\in[2n]
, and set
T^{\prime}:=T\xleftarrow{\mathrm{B}}x
,
\nu^{\prime}:=\operatorname{sh}(T^{\prime})
,
T^{\prime\prime}:=T^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\mathrm{B}}x^{\prime\prime}
,
\nu^{\prime\prime}:=\operatorname{sh}(T^{\prime\prime})
.
Let
r,r^{\prime}
denote the terminal rows of the Berele row-insertion for
(T,x)
and
(T^{\prime},x^{\prime})
, respectively.

(1)

If
x\leq x^{\prime}
, then we have
r\geq r^{\prime}

(2)

If
x>x^{\prime}
, then we have
r<r^{\prime}

Proof.

The first assertion has been proved in
[
Sun86
, Lemma 10.4]
.

Let us prove the second assertion.
Suppose that
\nu\supset\nu^{\prime}
.
Let
(c_{1},\dots,c_{s-1};\gamma_{s-1},\dots,\gamma_{k})
denote the Berele row-insertion route for
(T,x)
and
(x_{1},\dots,x_{s},\dots)
the row-inserting letters for
(T,x)
.
Then, we have

T^{\prime}(i,j)=\begin{cases}x_{i}&\text{ if }i\in[s-2]\text{ and }j=c_{i},\\
2s-3&\text{ if }i=s-1\text{ and }j\in[c_{s-1}-1],\\
T(s-1,j+1)&\text{ if }i=s-1\text{ and }j\in[c_{s-1},\gamma_{s-1}-1],\\
T(i,j+1)&\text{ if }i\in[s,k]\text{ and }j\in[\gamma_{i-1},\gamma_{i}-1],\\
T(i+1,j)&\text{ if }i\in[s-1,k-1]\text{ and }j=\gamma_{i},\\
T(i,j)&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

for all
(i,j)\in D(\nu^{\prime})
.

Set
S:=T^{\prime}\leftarrow x^{\prime}
, and let
(c^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,c^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime\prime}})
and
(x^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,x^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime\prime}})
denote the row-insertion route and the row-inserting letters for
(T^{\prime},x^{\prime})
, respectively.
Then, we have

x^{\prime\prime}_{1}=x^{\prime}<x=x_{1}

and

c^{\prime\prime}_{1}=\min\{j\geq 1\mid T^{\prime}(1,j)>x^{\prime\prime}_{1}\}.

Since
T^{\prime}(1,c_{1})=x_{1}>x^{\prime\prime}_{1}
, it holds that
c^{\prime\prime}_{1}\leq c_{1}
.
Proceeding in this way, one can deduce that

(2)

\displaystyle x^{\prime\prime}_{s-1}<x_{s-1}=2s-3=2(s-1)-1.

By Lemma
4.2.3
(
2
), this implies that
\nu^{\prime}\supset\nu^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
(c^{\prime}_{1},\dots,c^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}-1};\gamma^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}-1},\dots,\gamma^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})
denote the Berele row-insertion route for
(T^{\prime},x^{\prime})
.
By inequality (
2
), we see that

s^{\prime}<s.

Let us show that
\gamma_{i}\leq\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in[s-1,k]\cap[s^{\prime}-1,k^{\prime}]
.
Assume contrary that
\gamma_{i}>\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
for some
i
.
We may further assume that this
i
is the minimum among those.
Since
s^{\prime}<s
, we must have
i\geq s^{\prime}
.
By the minimality of
i
, it holds that
\gamma_{i-1}\leq\gamma^{\prime}_{i-1}
, where we set
\gamma_{s-2}:=1
.
Hence, by Proposition
3.4.3
(
1
), we have

\gamma_{i-1}\leq\gamma^{\prime}_{i-1}\leq\gamma^{\prime}_{i}<\gamma_{i}.

Then, we obtain

\displaystyle T^{\prime\prime}(i-1,\gamma^{\prime}_{i-1})=T^{\prime}(i,\gamma^{\prime}_{i-1})=T(i,\gamma^{\prime}_{i-1}+1),

\displaystyle T^{\prime\prime}(i-1,\gamma^{\prime}_{i-1}+1)=T^{\prime}(i-1,\gamma^{\prime}_{i-1}+1)=T(i-1,\gamma^{\prime}_{i-1}+1).

However, these contradict that
T^{\prime\prime}
and
T
are semistandard:

T^{\prime\prime}(i-1,\gamma^{\prime}_{i-1})\leq T^{\prime\prime}(i-1,\gamma^{\prime}_{i-1}),\quad T(i,\gamma^{\prime}_{i-1}+1)>T(i-1,\gamma^{\prime}_{i-1}+1).

Next, let us prove that
k>k^{\prime}
.
Assume contrary that
k\leq k^{\prime}
.
We have proved that
\gamma_{k}\leq\gamma^{\prime}_{k}
.
On the other hand, by Lemma
4.2.3
(
2
), we must have
\gamma_{k}=\nu_{k}=\nu^{\prime}_{k}+1
.
However, Proposition
3.4.3
(
2
) implies that
\gamma^{\prime}_{k}\leq\nu^{\prime}_{k}
.
Therefore, we obtain a contradiction.

So far, we have proved the following.
If
\nu\supset\nu^{\prime}
, then we have
\nu^{\prime}\supset\nu^{\prime\prime}
.
Moreover, it holds that
r=\bar{k}<\bar{k^{\prime}}=r^{\prime}
.

Now, assume that
\nu^{\prime}\subset\nu^{\prime\prime}
.
In this case, we must have
\nu\subset\nu^{\prime}
, for otherwise what we just stated yields
\nu^{\prime}\supset\nu^{\prime\prime}
.
By Lemma
4.2.3
(
1
), we obtain

T^{\prime}=T\leftarrow x,\quad T^{\prime\prime}=T^{\prime}\leftarrow x^{\prime}.

Then, the assertion follows from
[
Ful97
, §1.1 Row Bumping Lemma (2)]
.

It remains to consider the case when
\nu\subset\nu^{\prime}\supset\nu^{\prime\prime}
.
By Lemma
4.2.3
, we see that
r
is unbarred and
r^{\prime}
is barred.
Hence, we obtain
r<r^{\prime}
.
Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

5.
Row-insertion of type
A\mathrm{II}

In this section, we briefly recall results in
[
Wat25
]
, and introduce the row-insertion of type
A\mathrm{II}
.
Then, we show that it coincides with the Berele row-insertion.

Let
q
be an indeterminate, and

\mathbf{A}_{\infty}:=\mathbb{Q}[\![q^{-1}]\!]\cap\mathbb{Q}(q)

denote the ring of rational functions that are regular at
q=\infty
.
Define an equivalence relation
\equiv_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{Q}(q)
by declaring
f\equiv_{\infty}g
to mean that
f-g\in q^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{\infty}
.

5.1.
Quantum groups

Let
\mathbf{U}
denote the quantum group of
\mathfrak{gl}_{2n}
(
[
Lus93
, 3.1.1 and Corollary 33.1.5]
).
Namely, it is the unital associative
\mathbb{Q}(q)
-algebra with generators

\{E_{i},F_{i},D_{k}^{\pm 1}\mid i\in[2n-1],\ k\in[2n]\}

subject to the following relations:
for each
i,j\in[2n-1]
and
k,l\in[2n]
, we have

\displaystyle D_{k}D_{k}^{-1}=D_{k}^{-1}D_{k}=1,

\displaystyle D_{k}D_{l}=D_{l}D_{k},

\displaystyle D_{k}E_{i}=q^{\delta_{k,i}-\delta_{k,i+1}}E_{i}D_{k},

\displaystyle D_{k}F_{i}=q^{-\delta_{k,i}+\delta_{k,i+1}}F_{i}D_{k},

\displaystyle E_{i}F_{j}-F_{j}E_{i}=\delta_{i,j}\frac{K_{i}-K_{i}^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}},

\displaystyle E_{i}E_{j}=E_{j}E_{i}\quad\text{ if }|i-j|>1,

\displaystyle F_{i}F_{j}=F_{j}F_{i}\quad\text{ if }|i-j|>1,

\displaystyle E_{i}^{2}E_{j}-(q+q^{-1})E_{i}E_{j}E_{i}+E_{j}E_{i}^{2}=0\quad\text{ if }|i-j|=1,

\displaystyle F_{i}^{2}F_{j}-(q+q^{-1})F_{i}F_{j}F_{i}+F_{j}F_{i}^{2}=0\quad\text{ if }|i-j|=1,

where

K_{i}:=D_{i}D_{i+1}^{-1}.

Let
\rho
denote the anti-algebra involution on
\mathbf{U}
(
[
Lus93
, 19.1.1]
) defined by

\rho(E_{i}):=q^{-1}F_{i}K_{i},\ \rho(F_{i}):=qK_{i}^{-1}E_{i},\ \rho(D_{k}):=D_{k},\quad\text{ for all }i\in[2n-1],\ k\in[2n].

A symmetric bilinear form
(,)
on a
\mathbf{U}
-module is said to be
contragredient
if

(xu,v)=(u,\rho(x)v)\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbf{U},\ u,v\in M.

A basis
B
of a
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
equipped with a contragredient bilinear form
(,)
is said to be
almost orthonormal
if

(b_{1},b_{2})\equiv_{\infty}\delta_{b_{1},b_{2}}\quad\text{ for all }b_{1},b_{2}\in B.

Given a
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
equipped with a contragredient bilinear form
(,)
and an almost orthonormal basis
B
, we define an equivalence relation
\equiv_{\infty}
on
M
by declaring
u\equiv_{\infty}v
to mean

(u,b)\equiv_{\infty}(v,b)\quad\text{ for all }b\in B.

The quantum group
\mathbf{U}
is equipped with a Hopf algebra structure as in
[
Lus93
, §3.3]
.
Let
M,N
be
\mathbf{U}
-modules equipped with contragredient bilinear forms
(,)_{M}
,
(,)_{N}
and almost orthonormal bases
B_{M}
,
B_{N}
, respectively.
Then, the tensor product module
M\otimes N
has a contragredient bilinear form
(,)
defined by

(m_{1}\otimes n_{1},m_{2}\otimes n_{2}):=(m_{1},m_{2})_{M}\cdot(n_{1},n_{2})_{N}\quad\text{ for all }m_{1},m_{2}\in M,\ n_{1},n_{2}\in N.

The basis
\{b\otimes b^{\prime}\mid b\in B_{M},\ b^{\prime}\in B_{N}\}
of
M\otimes N
is almost orthonormal with respect to this bilinear form.

For each
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
, there exists a unique finite-dimensional simple
\mathbf{U}
-module
V(\lambda)
of highest weight
\lambda
.
It has an almost orthonormal basis, called the
canonical basis
(
cf
.
[
Lus93
, Definition 14.4.12]
), of the form

\{b_{T}\mid T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)\}.

In what follows, we often identify each element
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(1)
with its unique entry
T(1,1)\in[2n]
.
In particular, the basis elements of
V(1)
are denoted by
b_{x}
with
x\in[2n]
.

Theorem 5.1.1

(
cf
.
[
Kwo09
]
)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
.

(1)

There exists a
\mathbf{U}
-module isomorphism

\mathrm{RI}:V(\lambda)\otimes V(1)\to\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}\\
\mu\supset\lambda,\ |\mu/\lambda|=1\end{subarray}}V(\mu)

such that

\mathrm{RI}(b_{T}\otimes b_{x})\equiv_{\infty}b_{T\leftarrow x}\quad\text{ for all }T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda),\ x\in[2n].

(2)

Set
N:=|\lambda|
.
There exists an injective
\mathbf{U}
-module homomorphism

\mathrm{RR}:V(\lambda)\to V(1)^{\otimes N}

satisfying the following for all
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}
:
if
w_{\mathrm{row}}(T)=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
, then we have

\mathrm{RR}(T)\equiv_{\infty}b_{x_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{x_{N}}.

5.2.
Quantum symmetric pairs

For each
i\in[n-1]
, set

B_{2i}:=F_{2i}-q[E_{2i-1},[E_{2i+1},E_{2i}]_{q^{-1}}]_{q^{-1}}K_{2i}^{-1}\in\mathbf{U},

where
[,]_{q^{-1}}
denotes the
q^{-1}
-commutator:

[x,y]_{q^{-1}}:=xy-q^{-1}yx.

Let
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
denote the subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}
generated by

\{E_{2j-1},F_{2j-1},K_{2j-1}^{\pm 1}\mid i\in[n]\}\sqcup\{B_{2k}\mid k\in[n-1]\}.

The pair
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{U}^{\imath})
forms a quantum symmetric pair of type
A\mathrm{II}_{2n-1}
.

The subalgebra
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
is invariant under the involution
\rho

[
BaWa18
, Proposition 4.6]
.
Hence, we can define the notions of contragredient bilinear forms and almost orthonormal bases, and equivalence relations
\equiv_{\infty}
on modules, just as in quantum group.

Since
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
is a subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}
, each
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
can be regarded as a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module by restriction.
If
(,)
is a contragredient bilinear form of the
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
, then it is also a contragredient bilinear form of the
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
M
.

The subalgebra
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
is a right coideal of
\mathbf{U}
.
Hence, given a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
M
and a
\mathbf{U}
-module
N
, we can equip the tensor product
M\otimes N
with a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module structure.

For each
\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
, there exists a unique, up to isomorphism, simple weight
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V^{\imath}(\nu)
of highest weight
\nu
(
[
Mol06
, Theorem 6.3]
; see also
[
Wat21
, Proposition 3.3.9 and Corollary 4.3.2]
).

Proposition 5.2.1

(
[
Wat25
, Proposition 6.4.1]
)
.

Let
\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
.
Then, there exists an almost orthonormal basis of
V^{\imath}(\nu)
of the form

\{b^{\imath}_{T}\mid T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)\}

and a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism
p_{\nu}:V(\nu)\to V^{\imath}(\nu)
such that

p_{\nu}(b_{T})\equiv_{\infty}\begin{cases}b^{\imath}_{T}&\text{ if }T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu),\\
0&\text{ if }T\notin Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu),\end{cases}

for all
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\nu)
.

Proposition 5.2.2
.

Let
\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
.
Then, there exists an injective
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism

\mathrm{For}:V^{\imath}(\nu)\to V(\nu)

such that

\mathrm{For}(b^{\imath}_{T})\equiv_{\infty}b_{T}\quad\text{ for all }T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu).

Proof.

The
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V(\nu)
is semisimple since it is equipped with a nondegenerate contragredient bilinear form.
Hence, the assertion follows from Proposition
5.2.1
.
∎

5.3.
Littlewood–Richardson maps

In this subsection, we fix
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
.

Given a word
w=(x_{1},\dots,x_{l})\in\mathcal{W}_{2n}
and integers
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, set

w[a,b]:=(w_{a},w_{a+1},\dots,w_{b}),\quad w[a]:=w[1,a].

Let
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
.
Let
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{l})
denote the first column of
T
(read from the top to bottom), and
S
the remaining part:

l:=\ell(\lambda),\quad a_{i}:=T(i,1),\quad S(i,j):=T(i,j+1).

Define a subword
\operatorname{rem}(\mathbf{a})\subseteq\mathbf{a}
by the following recursive formula:

\operatorname{rem}(\mathbf{a}):=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\text{ if }l\leq 1,\\
\mathrm{rem}(\mathbf{a}[l-2])\cdot\mathbf{a}[l-1,l]&\text{ if }l\geq 2,\ a_{l}\in 2\mathbb{Z},\ a_{l-1}=a_{l}-1,\text{ and }\\
&\phantom{if\ }a_{l}<2l-|\mathrm{rem}(\mathbf{a}[l-2])|-1,\\
\mathrm{rem}(\mathbf{a}[l-1])&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Also, set
\operatorname{red}(\mathbf{a})
to be the subword of
\mathbf{a}
consisting of letters not in
\operatorname{rem}(\mathbf{a})
,
and
\operatorname{suc}(T)
to be the unique semistandard tableau whose row-word is Knuth equivalent to
\operatorname{red}(\mathbf{a})^{\mathrm{rev}}\cdot w_{\mathrm{col}}(S)
, where
\mathrm{red}(\mathbf{a})^{\mathrm{rev}}
denotes the reverse of
\mathrm{red}(\mathbf{a})
.

Lemma 5.3.2

(
[
Wat25
, Corollary 4.3.8]
)
.

For each
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
, the following are equivalent.

(1)

T
is symplectic.

(2)

\mathrm{suc}(T)=T
.

Definition 5.3.3

(
[
Wat25
, §3.1]
)
.

The
Littlewood–Richardson map
of type
A\mathrm{II}
is the algorithm which takes
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
as input, and outputs a pair
(P(T),Q(T))
of tableaux as follows.

(1)

Initialize
t:=0
,
P^{t}:=T
,
\nu^{t}:=\operatorname{sh}(P^{t})
, and
Q^{t}
to be the unique skew tableau of shape
\lambda/\lambda
.

(2)

Set
P^{t+1}:=\mathrm{suc}(P^{t})
,
\nu^{t+1}:=\operatorname{sh}(P^{t+1})
, and
Q^{t+1}
to be the skew tableau of shape
\lambda/\nu^{t+1}
defined by

Q^{t+1}(i,j):=\begin{cases}Q^{t}(i,j)&\text{ if }(i,j)\notin D(\nu^{t}),\\
t+1&\text{ if }(i,j)\in D(\nu^{t}),\end{cases}

for each
(i,j)\in D(\lambda/\nu^{t+1})
.

(3)

(a)

If
P^{t+1}=P^{t}
, then return
(P^{t},Q^{t})
.

(b)

Otherwise, increment
t
, and then go back to step (
2
).

Set

\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu):=\{Q(T)\mid T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)\text{ such that }\operatorname{sh}(P(T))=\nu\}.

An explicit description of the set
\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
can be found in
[
Wat25
, Theorem 3.1.4 (2)]
.

Theorem 5.3.4

(
[
Wat25
, Theorem 3.1.4 (1)]
)
.

The Littlewood–Richardson map of type
A\mathrm{II}
gives rise to a bijection

\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)\to\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}\\
\nu\subseteq\lambda\end{subarray}}(Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)\times\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)).

Theorem 5.3.5

(
[
Wat25
, Theorem 7.2.1]
)
.

There exists a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module isomorphism

\mathrm{LR}:V(\lambda)\to\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}\\
\nu\subseteq\lambda\end{subarray}}(V^{\imath}(\nu)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(q)\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu))

such that

\mathrm{LR}(b_{T})\equiv_{\infty}b^{\imath}_{P(T)}\otimes Q(T)\quad\text{ for all }T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda).

Here, the linear space
\mathbb{Q}(q)\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
is regarded as a
\mathbf{U}
-module by the trivial action, and equipped with an almost orthonormal basis
\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda,\nu)
.

5.4.
Row-insertion of type
A\mathrm{II}

In this subsection, we fix
\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
.

Based on the representation theoretical interpretation of the row-insertion (Theorem
5.1.1
(
1
)), it is natural to define the row-insertion of type
A\mathrm{II}
as follows.

Definition 5.4.1
.

The
row-insertion of type
A\mathrm{II}
is the procedure which transforms a pair
(T,x)\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)\times[2n]
into a new symplectic tableau
T\xleftarrow{A\mathrm{II}}x
by

T\xleftarrow{A\mathrm{II}}x:=P(T\leftarrow x).

0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$9$\hss}\vss}}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule 690.4pt}\xrightarrow{P(\cdot)}\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule
